People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It starts with something small, like a client not responding, and then pretty quickly I’m thinking I’m going to lose the contract, fall behind on divorce payments, and… never get back to a real leadership role.\n\nDoctor: Was there something in particular that happened recently that started these racing thoughts for you?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t one single thing this week, exactly. It’s… been going on since I got fired, about thirteen months ago. Even after I picked up contract work, it just didn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or places because of these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I don’t go to soccer much anymore, and I’ve kind of stopped reaching out to friends. It’s embarrassing—I’m tense, I snap at people.\n\nWork-wise, I’ve put off networking follow-ups and even delayed some client stuff because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since you got fired?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not most of the time, exactly. It’s more like I feel worn down and frustrated. I wouldn’t say sad or empty all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Most of the day, honestly. I feel kind of wound up… like my shoulders won’t unclench, and I’m just waiting for something bad to happen. I’ll pace around or bounce my leg on calls, even when there’s no real reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any changes in your appetite or sleep pattern since you got fired?\n\nPatient: Sleep, yeah. It’s been pretty bad. Most nights I’m lying there going over interview answers in my head, or I wake up and it’s 2 or 3 a.m. and I’m checking the clock. Even when I technically sleep enough, I wake up feeling like I was on call all night.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of why I came in. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying, and then I can’t stop it. Pretty soon I’m thinking I’ll lose the contract, fall behind on divorce payments, and… I don’t know, never get back into a real leadership role. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts on purpose or is it something that's happening without you wanting it to?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel on purpose. It’s embarrassing—I’m tense, I snap at people.\n\nWork-wise, I’ve put off networking follow-ups and even delayed some client stuff because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since you got fired?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not most of the time, exactly. It’s more like I feel worn down and frustrated. I wouldn’t say sad or empty all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Most of the day, honestly. I feel kind of wound up… like my shoulders won’t unclench, and I’m just waiting for something bad to happen. I’ll pace around or bounce my leg on calls, even when there’s no real reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any changes in your appetite or sleep pattern since you got fired?\n\nPatient: Sleep, yeah. It’s been pretty bad. Most nights I’m lying there going over interview answers in my head, or I wake up and it’s 2 or 3 a.m. and I’m checking the clock. Even when I technically sleep enough, I wake up feeling like I was on call all night.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of why I came in. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying, and then I can’t stop it. Pretty soon I’m thinking I’ll lose the contract, fall behind on divorce payments, and… I don’t know, never get back into a real leadership role. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts on purpose or is it something that's happening without you wanting it to?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel on purpose. It’s more like… once it starts, I can’t shut it off. One client doesn’t answer and my head just runs with it—losing the contract, money stuff, not getting back to where I was. I know it sounds like a lot, but it happens fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and calm your thoughts or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might double-check an email or something, but not… over and over like that. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s usually not just one thing. My mind jumps around—from contract work, to the mortgage, to… whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. Even something like a recruiter taking too long to email back can feel like… proof everything’s closing down career-wise.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or events that you avoid because you fear something bad will happen if you participate in them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some things, I guess. I’ve put off networking follow-ups, and even delayed client work because I start thinking through every way it could go badly.\n\nAnd I’ve kind of pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Not because I think something specific will happen, exactly… more because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine presence of traumatic event", "question": "Was your firing from the job thirteen months ago unexpected or particularly traumatic for you?"}

Even something like a recruiter taking too long to email back can feel like… proof everything’s closing down career-wise.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or events that you avoid because you fear something bad will happen if you participate in them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some things, I guess. I’ve put off networking follow-ups, and even delayed client work because I start thinking through every way it could go badly.\n\nAnd I’ve kind of pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Not because I think something specific will happen, exactly… more because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Was your firing from the job thirteen months ago unexpected or particularly traumatic for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it traumatic. It was… a shock, yeah. I didn’t see it coming the way it happened. It starts with something small, like a client not responding, and then pretty quickly I’m thinking I’m going to lose the contract, fall behind on divorce payments, and… never get back to a real leadership role.\n\nDoctor: Was there something in particular that happened recently that started these racing thoughts for you?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t one single thing this week, exactly. It’s… been going on since I got fired, about thirteen months ago. Even after I picked up contract work, it just didn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or places because of these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I don’t go to soccer much anymore, and I’ve kind of stopped reaching out to friends. It’s embarrassing—I’m tense, I snap at people.\n\nWork-wise, I’ve put off networking follow-ups and even delayed some client stuff because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since you got fired?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not most of the time, exactly. It’s more like I feel worn down and frustrated. I wouldn’t say sad or empty all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Most of the day, honestly. I feel kind of wound up… like my shoulders won’t unclench, and I’m just waiting for something bad to happen. I’ll pace around or bounce my leg on calls, even when there’s no real reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any changes in your appetite or sleep pattern since you got fired?\n\nPatient: Sleep, yeah. It’s been pretty bad. Most nights I’m lying there going over interview answers in my head, or I wake up and it’s 2 or 3 a.m. and I’m checking the clock. Even when I technically sleep enough, I wake up feeling like I was on call all night.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of why I came in. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying, and then I can’t stop it. Pretty soon I’m thinking I’ll lose the contract, fall behind on divorce payments, and… I don’t know, never get back into a real leadership role. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts on purpose or is it something that's happening without you wanting it to?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel on purpose. It’s more like… once it starts, I can’t shut it off. One client doesn’t answer and my head just runs with it—losing the contract, money stuff, not getting back to where I was. I know it sounds like a lot, but it happens fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and calm your thoughts or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might double-check an email or something, but not… over and over like that. It was… a shock, yeah. I didn’t see it coming the way it happened. It starts with something small, like a client not responding, and then pretty quickly I’m thinking I’m going to lose the contract, fall behind on divorce payments, and… never get back to a real leadership role.\n\nDoctor: Was there something in particular that happened recently that started these racing thoughts for you?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t one single thing this week, exactly. It’s… been going on since I got fired, about thirteen months ago. Even after I picked up contract work, it just didn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or places because of these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I don’t go to soccer much anymore, and I’ve kind of stopped reaching out to friends. It’s embarrassing—I’m tense, I snap at people.\n\nWork-wise, I’ve put off networking follow-ups and even delayed some client stuff because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since you got fired?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not most of the time, exactly. It’s more like I feel worn down and frustrated. I wouldn’t say sad or empty all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Most of the day, honestly. I feel kind of wound up… like my shoulders won’t unclench, and I’m just waiting for something bad to happen. I’ll pace around or bounce my leg on calls, even when there’s no real reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any changes in your appetite or sleep pattern since you got fired?\n\nPatient: Sleep, yeah. It’s been pretty bad. Most nights I’m lying there going over interview answers in my head, or I wake up and it’s 2 or 3 a.m. and I’m checking the clock. Even when I technically sleep enough, I wake up feeling like I was on call all night.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of why I came in. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying, and then I can’t stop it. Pretty soon I’m thinking I’ll lose the contract, fall behind on divorce payments, and… I don’t know, never get back into a real leadership role. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts on purpose or is it something that's happening without you wanting it to?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel on purpose. It’s more like… once it starts, I can’t shut it off. One client doesn’t answer and my head just runs with it—losing the contract, money stuff, not getting back to where I was. I know it sounds like a lot, but it happens fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and calm your thoughts or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might double-check an email or something, but not… over and over like that. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s usually not just one thing. Even something like a recruiter taking too long to email back can feel like… proof everything’s closing down career-wise.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or events that you avoid because you fear something bad will happen if you participate in them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some things, I guess. I’ve put off networking follow-ups, and even delayed client work because I start thinking through every way it could go badly.\n\nAnd I’ve kind of pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Not because I think something specific will happen, exactly… more because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Was your firing from the job thirteen months ago unexpected or particularly traumatic for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it traumatic. It was… a shock, yeah. I didn’t see it coming the way it happened. But I don’t really know how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Did your excessive worry and tension start immediately after you were fired, or was there a delay before they became a problem?\n\nPatient: It was pretty much after I was fired. I don’t think there was much of a delay. It’s been going on since then… about thirteen months now, and even getting contract work didn’t really make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't worry excessively about different things?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Since I was fired, about thirteen months ago, it’s just kind of been there. Even after I got some contract work, it didn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I’ve missed some networking follow-ups, and I’ve put off client deliverables because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong. And I’ve kind of backed away from soccer and seeing friends. I’m… embarrassed, I guess, by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your firing thirteen months ago as traumatic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it traumatic. It was… really upsetting, and it shook me up, but I’m not sure that’s the word I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Is your excessive worry usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Mostly ordinary stuff, I guess. Work, money… what people in my field think of me now. Even when I technically sleep enough, I wake up feeling like I was on call all night.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of why I came in. It’ll start with one thing, like a client not replying, and then I can’t stop it. Pretty soon I’m thinking I’ll lose the contract, fall behind on divorce payments, and… I don’t know, never get back into a real leadership role. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts on purpose or is it something that's happening without you wanting it to?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel on purpose. It’s more like… once it starts, I can’t shut it off. One client doesn’t answer and my head just runs with it—losing the contract, money stuff, not getting back to where I was. I know it sounds like a lot, but it happens fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and calm your thoughts or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might double-check an email or something, but not… over and over like that. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s usually not just one thing. My mind jumps around—from contract work, to the mortgage, to… whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. Even something like a recruiter taking too long to email back can feel like… proof everything’s closing down career-wise.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or events that you avoid because you fear something bad will happen if you participate in them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some things, I guess. I’ve put off networking follow-ups, and even delayed client work because I start thinking through every way it could go badly.\n\nAnd I’ve kind of pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Not because I think something specific will happen, exactly… more because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Was your firing from the job thirteen months ago unexpected or particularly traumatic for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it traumatic. It was… a shock, yeah. I didn’t see it coming the way it happened. But I don’t really know how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Did your excessive worry and tension start immediately after you were fired, or was there a delay before they became a problem?\n\nPatient: It was pretty much after I was fired. I don’t think there was much of a delay. It’s been going on since then… about thirteen months now, and even getting contract work didn’t really make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't worry excessively about different things?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Since I was fired, about thirteen months ago, it’s just kind of been there. Even after I got some contract work, it didn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I’ve missed some networking follow-ups, and I’ve put off client deliverables because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong. And I’ve kind of backed away from soccer and seeing friends. I’m… embarrassed, I guess, by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your firing thirteen months ago as traumatic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it traumatic. It was… really upsetting, and it shook me up, but I’m not sure that’s the word I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Is your excessive worry usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Mostly ordinary stuff, I guess. Work, money… what people in my field think of me now. My mind jumps from a contract deadline to the mortgage to whether a recruiter not emailing back means my career is basically over. It gets… bigger than it should.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen during your firing that made it particularly distressing for you?\n\nPatient: Not one thing, really. It was just… the way it happened, I guess. Sudden, and humiliating. I don’t really want to get into all the details.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s… been going on since I got fired, about thirteen months ago. It was… a shock, yeah. I didn’t see it coming the way it happened. Since I was fired, about thirteen months ago, it’s just kind of been there. I’m… embarrassed, I guess, by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your firing thirteen months ago as traumatic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it traumatic. It was… really upsetting, and it shook me up, but I’m not sure that’s the word I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Is your excessive worry usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Mostly ordinary stuff, I guess. Work, money… what people in my field think of me now. My mind jumps from a contract deadline to the mortgage to whether a recruiter not emailing back means my career is basically over. It was just… the way it happened, I guess. Sudden, and humiliating. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about multiple things at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s usually not just one thing. My mind jumps around—from contract work, to the mortgage, to… whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. Even something like a recruiter taking too long to email back can feel like… proof everything’s closing down career-wise.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or events that you avoid because you fear something bad will happen if you participate in them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some things, I guess. I’ve put off networking follow-ups, and even delayed client work because I start thinking through every way it could go badly.\n\nAnd I’ve kind of pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Not because I think something specific will happen, exactly… more because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Was your firing from the job thirteen months ago unexpected or particularly traumatic for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it traumatic. It was… a shock, yeah. I didn’t see it coming the way it happened. But I don’t really know how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Did your excessive worry and tension start immediately after you were fired, or was there a delay before they became a problem?\n\nPatient: It was pretty much after I was fired. I don’t think there was much of a delay. It’s been going on since then… about thirteen months now, and even getting contract work didn’t really make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't worry excessively about different things?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Since I was fired, about thirteen months ago, it’s just kind of been there. Even after I got some contract work, it didn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I’ve missed some networking follow-ups, and I’ve put off client deliverables because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong. And I’ve kind of backed away from soccer and seeing friends. I’m… embarrassed, I guess, by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your firing thirteen months ago as traumatic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it traumatic. It was… really upsetting, and it shook me up, but I’m not sure that’s the word I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Is your excessive worry usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Mostly ordinary stuff, I guess. Work, money… what people in my field think of me now. My mind jumps from a contract deadline to the mortgage to whether a recruiter not emailing back means my career is basically over. It gets… bigger than it should.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen during your firing that made it particularly distressing for you?\n\nPatient: Not one thing, really. It was just… the way it happened, I guess. Sudden, and humiliating. I don’t really want to get into all the details.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these excessive worries for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Since I was fired, so… about thirteen months now. It didn’t really stop, even after I picked up contract work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying excessively about everyday things for most days over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most days, yeah. It’s the regular stuff, but it doesn’t stay regular in my head. Contract work, mortgage payments… whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods.
